#7073. Education Department faces management challenges from recent changes, watchdog says

Let Higher Ed Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

An annual federal report cited interagency agreements and a handful of executive orders as key factors that will challenge the agency in 2027.

Since early 2025, the second Trump administration has outsourced many of the Education Department’s core functions such as grant management, monitoring and technical assistance to other agencies through interagency agreements. The department has also reorganized many of its offices and laid off a bulk of its staff.

"Change of this magnitude can result in challenges associated with employee resistance, fatigue, and uncertainty — such as decreased productivity, operational disruption, and lost efficiency — and have an impact on the organization’s ability to respond to stakeholder questions and concerns," the Nov. 13 report from OIG stated.

The Education Department has not yet issued an annual performance plan for fiscal year 2026 and has yet to issue its annual performance report for FY 2025, strategic plan for FY 2026-2030, or annual performance plan for FY 2027, according to the report.

Other reports through various federal offices have flagged similar concerns with the Education Department's output amid McMahon's efforts to close the agency, which ultimately requires Congressional approval.

In June, another OIG report found that layoffs at the Education Department appear to have impacted units that were performing legal duties.

The staffing changes were made to 16 of the department's 17 offices, resulting in an overall reduction of at least 40% of the more than 4,000 employees who were working for the Education Department when Trump reentered office in January 2025.

Many lawmakers, civil rights groups and lawyers have raised concerns with output from at least one of those offices, the Office for Civil Rights, which saw a slowdown in case resolutions that included no sexual harassment or assault cases being settled in 2025. A later report from the office of Sen. Bernie Sanders, I-Vt., showed that OCR resolved only 1% of its cases overall in 2025, the lowest percentage from any OCR in over the last decade.

Responding to a draft of the most recent OIG report, the Education Department said it had made progress in many of the areas of concern, including change management. According to OIG, the department said it has "continued efforts to improve operational processes and maintain program delivery during a period of substantial organizational change." Additionally, the agency said it has "engaged in contractor support to assist with resolution for external audits," among other adjustments.

The department has previously maintained that its staffing and organizational changes will not affect the statutory functions it is legally obliged to perform.

The department also stressed in a comment to K-12 Dive that the OIG report is an annual update. In a January OIG report, the office flagged similar oversight challenges stemming from executive orders and resulting organizational changes.

"It is critical that the Department strategically plan for and effectively manage these and other major operational changes that it may decide to undertake," it warned at the time.

Past reports have flagged challenges under previous administrations, such as monitoring grantees' use of COVID-19 pandemic relief funding.

#7071. Districts put off tough decisions in face of enrollment declines

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

As of 2024-25, post-COVID enrollment declined but staffing and the number of operating schools increased, Stanford researchers found.

The study does not include national data from the 2025-26 and 2026-27 school years, when school districts have finalized more school closure and consolidation decisions.

Still, the Stanford researchers said the misalignment between enrollment, the number of operating schools and staffing seen so far since the pandemic is likely due to the local political environments in which districts are making their decisions.

“The prospect of school closures and layoffs constitutes a highly visible and consequential threat to parents and teachers who can readily lobby school-board members and district leadership,” the study authors said. “That pressure, coupled with the often brief and provisional nature of superintendent tenures, can forestall confronting difficult decisions.”

A separate report released Tuesday by the Reason Foundation, a libertarian think tank, found that among nine of the nation’s 12 largest public school systems, there were almost 938,000 empty seats during the 2025-26 school year. Those nine districts, on average, only filled about 71% of their student capacity.

The district with the most underutilized capacity was the Los Angeles Unified School District, which had 365,410 empty seats with a 49% utilization rate, according to the Reason Foundation.

LAUSD leadership has hinted that closures and consolidations could be considered in the near future given the district’s drastic enrollment declines over the past two decades.

This fall, LAUSD Superintendent Andrés Chait addressed the district’s enrollment trends in a video shared with school community members. Chait noted that enrollment is nearly half of what it used to be 20 years ago. Even so, he said, LAUSD currently operates almost the same number of school campuses.

Similar to the Stanford analysis, the Reason Foundation report acknowledged that school closures can be difficult decisions for students and local communities. “But public school enrollment declines and districts’ financial situations are unlikely to improve, so these challenges must be addressed,” the Reason report said.

If districts cannot properly “right-size” the number of schools they operate, the Stanford analysis said, consequential spending cuts will occur elsewhere, such as building maintenance or support services for student mental health and instruction. And if districts continue to struggle with properly managing their finances, they may be more likely to face a state takeover, the Stanford researchers said.

#7065. Title IX sex bias employment case about to get its day in Supreme Court

Let K-12 Dive's free newsletter keep you informed, straight from your inbox.

A lower court said institutions may not know when accepting federal funds that they are accepting liability under Title IX, when Title VII typically covers employment claims.

School districts could get a hint of how the U.S. Supreme Court will rule on a case that could change how teachers and other school employees bring sex discrimination claims when the justices hear oral arguments next week.

In Thomas Crowther v. Board of Regents of the University System of Georgia, the 11th U.S. Court of Appeals ruled in 2024 that Title IX’s right to sue doesn’t apply to employees, diverging from at least eight other appeals courts. At odds is whether school employees have the right to sue privately under Title IX outside of protections under Title VII.

Title IX protects anyone who interacts with a school — including students, employees and parents — from sex-based discrimination in federally funded education programs and is often invoked by students who are harassed, assaulted or bullied due to their sex. Title IX protections for school employees include, for instance, prohibiting discrimination against teachers due to pregnancy or pregnancy-related conditions, among other things.

Meanwhile, Title VII prohibits employment discrimination based on race, color, religion, sex and national origin.

In Crowther, two University System of Georgia employees — a basketball coach at Georgia Tech and an art professor at Augusta University — said the university system discriminated against them on the basis of sex when it fired them.

Georgia Tech women’s basketball coach MaChelle Joseph was fired in 2019 shortly after she filed an internal complaint that alleged the university was discriminating against her and her program by not providing the same resources as the men’s program.

At Augusta University, art professor Thomas Crowther was told his contract would not be renewed in 2021 after several students reported allegedly inappropriate conduct in his class — including sexual harassment — which triggered a Title IX investigation. That investigation process and ensuing negative performance reviews, he said, discriminated against him on the basis of him being male.

Joseph and Crowther both reported the alleged discrimination to the U.S. Equal Employment Opportunity Commission, but also sued in the U.S. District Court for the Northern District of Georgia under Title IX.

The two cases were combined before the 11th Circuit, which said in its 2024 decision that schools may not understand when accepting federal funds that they accept potential liability for damages of Title IX claims by employees, when those types of claims are typically filed under Title VII.

The Association of Title IX Administrators called the Supreme Court case "one of the most consequential decisions impacting the intersection of Title IX and employment law in decades."

Multiple major education and legal organizations have filed briefs in the case, including the National Education Association and the National Women's Law Center.

"The American education system faces a severe and worsening crisis in educator recruitment and retention," said NEA's brief that was filed earlier this year in favor of the former University System of Georgia employees. "Anti-discrimination protections affect whether talented professionals enter, remain in, or depart from careers."

The NEA said that a court decision implicates "a nationwide educator workforce crisis, the professional environment in which recruitment and retention decisions are made, and ultimately the quality of education available to students across the country."

"When sex discrimination drives talented educators from the profession, the consequences extend beyond individual career harm and cause systemic degradation of educational quality," the teachers union said.

Granting teachers the right to sue privately under Title IX would help improve an environment where sex discrimination "has remained pervasive," the NWLC said in its own brief. The center cited teachers' high rates of sexual harassment from their colleagues and students, unequal pay for female teachers despite their overrepresentation in the profession, a lack of leadership opportunities, and pregnancy discrimination, such as a lack of accommodations and supportive school policies.

Such a decision would also help address lower investments in girls' school sports and barriers to STEM in education programs, NWLC said.

Title IX "can only be meaningful for women who work in education if they can enforce their right to be free from sex discrimination, including in court," the organization argued.

However, the Board of Regents of the University System of Georgia maintained in its opposing brief filed in October 2025 that Title IX does not include a private right to sue and that petitioners "had every chance to prove their claims for employment-based sex discrimination under Title VII."

"Had Congress desired that employees of educational institutions have a bonus remedy for employment discrimination under Title IX, unburdened by Title VII’s exhaustion requirements and damage caps, it could have provided as much," the respondents said. "It has not."

The case invokes another — Jackson v. Birmingham Board of Education, a 2005 Title IX case that was decided 5-4 in favor of the plaintiff.

That case was brought by Roderick Jackson, a high school girls’ basketball coach who spoke out to his supervisors after he noticed that his team was not receiving equal funding and equal access to athletic equipment and facilities. Following his complaints, he received negative work reviews and was ultimately removed from his position.

His lawsuit reached the high court, which decided that Title IX's private right to sue included retaliation claims for complaining about sex discrimination.

"Reporting incidents of discrimination is integral to Title IX enforcement and would be discouraged if retaliation against those who report went unpunished," the Supreme Court said in its decision. "Indeed, if retaliation were not prohibited, Title IX’s enforcement scheme would unravel.”

However, more than two decades later, Crowther will be heard by a much different bench, given the justices in the majority on the Jackson opinion have long since retired or died.

#7064. Quit saying “I’m not a math person”

It’s quite rare to hear someone say, “I can’t read” or “I’m illiterate so I don’t read.” On the other hand, lots of people claim that they’re “not a math person.” The phrase isn’t just “I don’t like math.”

#7063. Influencer & Media Manager at Yodo1

You will: Build Spicy Koala's reputation as a credible publisher and curator of premium indie PC and console games through effective PR, media relations, creator partnerships and community development.

Must have: A current network of influential media and content creators and demonstrable experience of managing marketing communications, as well as building gaming communities.

At Yodo1, we don't just build and grow games, we turn them into global successes that become household names.

With 14+ years in the games space, we've reached 3.5 billion+ players and supported 6,000+ developers worldwide. From games like Transformers: Earth Wars, Rodeo Stampede and Ski Safari, to bringing iconic IPs from Hasbro, AMC, Netflix, Paramount and Sony Pictures to leading studios like Rivergame, Candivore, 37Games and Tencent Studios, we connect creativity with global expertise.

Our work spans game development, publishing, IP licensing and ad monetization across mobile, PC and console. We build games, grow franchises and help developers reach players worldwide. We love games. We play games. And together, we grow games that last.

We believe in the power of games and that everyone deserves the opportunity to build a life they love, from anywhere in the world.

We're fully remote by design. We embrace Work From Anywhere, with no fixed hours or limits on time off. We trust our people to own their work and deliver results.

We're driven by performance, curiosity, and continuous improvement. We experiment often, challenge assumptions and value impact over titles.

Yodo1 is seeking an experienced, commercially minded Influencer & Media Manager with a track record of generating positive coverage via their current, active network of games media and content creators. Must be able to provide evidence of successfully establishing, growing and actively engaging player communities on Discord and Steam Communities.

Focused on impact. You understand the 80/20 rule and focus on activities that matter.

Performance-driven and growth-oriented. You have an intrinsic will to succeed while unafraid to try, fail, and learn by experimenting with new approaches and testing ideas.

Cooperative. You help others succeed; you give and receive feedback well.

Humble. You ask for help; you are quick to admit and learn from your mistakes.

SMART. You work and communicate in ways that are Specific, Measurable, Actionable, Relevant, and Time-bound.

Develop and execute PR and communications strategies for the publishing label and individual game releases, working closely in partnership with our Social Media Manager to effectively promote our gaming portfolio and achieve strong commercial launches.

Build and maintain relationships with games media, journalists, influencers and content creators to secure coverage, reviews, features and creator-led promotion.

Create compelling stories and narratives around our games, developers and releases, translating their distinctive qualities into effective press releases, announcements and promotional opportunities.

Plan and coordinate PR activity & creator campaigns around key milestones, including reveals, demos, Early Access launches and full releases.

Establish, grow and actively engage player communities on Discord and Steam Community, where appropriate, creating sustained engagement before and after release.

Work closely with developers and internal publishing, production and marketing teams to ensure consistent messaging, maximise visibility and respond to player feedback.

Monitor coverage, creator activity, community sentiment and campaign performance, using insights to improve reach and engagement.

An active, current network of games media contacts and content creators, with demonstrable evidence of having recently leveraged these relationships to secure coverage, exposure or content creation for games you have represented.

Practical, hands-on experience of planning, hosting and delivering Steam livestreams.

Excellent written and verbal communication skills, with experience producing press releases and communicating effectively with games media and content creators. Equally, must be comfortable establishing, developing and actively engaging gaming communities, primarily through Discord and Steam.

A strong understanding of the PC and console games market, indie developers and gaming audiences.

A proactive, hands-on approach, comfortable working across multiple games and release campaigns in a small, growing publishing team.

Compensation. We pay based on qualifications, experience, and fit for the role. We provide an allowance to purchase your own device or equipment and offer variable compensation through our annual bonus program.

Growth. We offer an environment where you will have the trust and freedom to try, fail, learn fast, and make a real impact while accelerating your career, and where anyone with a great idea can pitch it directly to our management team.

WFA. Work from anywhere in the world, travel when and where you like, get paid in your currency of choice, and enjoy flexible hours and unlimited paid time off.

Community. Work with an energetic, fast-learning, supportive, and diverse team of Yodies across six continents and 30+ countries who share and support new ideas and get things done.

New remote jobs delivered to your inbox, every week.

Location independence is the future of work. It serves companies with best talent from around the globe. And benefits employees with a greater work life balance and the ability to work in their pyjamas.- Nithur, founder.
